and that
\mathcal{H}_{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C})
holds for every
\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta
.
To prove that
\mathcal{H}_{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{c},\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{C})
holds,
we proceed scale by scale using
Propositions
3.5
–
3.7
.

We have already observed that
\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{C};x)
holds for every
|x|\leq 2\xi(0)
,
thanks to Proposition
3.5
.
For typical scales, Proposition
3.6
gives more than we need, as it says that
\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\tfrac{1}{8}\mathbf{C};x)
holds for every

|x|\geq\eta\xi(\beta)\vee 2\xi(0)
.
Finally, by Proposition
3.7
, for the remaining intermediate scale
2\xi(0)\leq|x|\leq\eta\xi(\beta)
, we have

F_{\beta}(x)\leq\frac{\exp(\mathbf{c}\eta)}{3}\cdot\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathbf{C}}{\sigma^{d}_{J}}\left(\frac{\sigma_{J}}{\sigma_{J}\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2-\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\mathbf{c}\frac{|x|}{\xi(\beta)}\right).

(3.18)

Since
\eta\leq 1
and
\mathbf{c}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\log 2
by assumption,
this proves that
\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\tfrac{1}{3}\mathbf{C};x)
holds
for the intermediate scale.
This concludes the proof, by setting
\delta_{1}:=\delta_{2}
.
∎

4
Preliminaries: integration, regularity, stability

In this section, we develop three preliminary ingredients
for the proof of Theorem
3.1
.
In Section
4.1
, we use elementary calculus to derive consequences of the upper and lower differential inequalities (
1.13
) and (

⁢
I
.2

) provided by Assumption
I
. This is the
only
place in the paper where (

⁢
I
.2

) is used. In Section
4.2
, we give a first application of the results of Section
4.1
and prove Proposition
2.2
. In
Section
4.3
, we combine
Section
4.1
and Corollary
2.9
to prove the regularity of the effective random walk
stated in Proposition
2.8
. Finally, in Section
4.4
, we prove the stability result
stated in Proposition
2.12
. It plays an important role in the proofs of Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
.

4.1
Integration of differential inequalities

Throughout Section
4.1
, we fix
d>2
and
we assume that
J
and
G
obey Assumption
I
.
Recall that
F_{\beta}=J*G_{\beta}
and

\chi(\beta)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}G_{\beta}(x)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(x)=\|F_{\beta}\|_{1},\quad E(\beta)=\max_{0\leq t\leq\beta}\left(\|H_{t}\|_{1}+\frac{\||x|_{2}^{2}\cdot H_{t}\|_{1}}{\xi(t)^{2}}\right).

(4.1)

It is only in this section that we use the definition of
E(\beta)
. Subsequently
we will only use the fact that
E(\beta)<\delta
for every
\beta<\beta(\delta)
, by the definition of
\beta(\delta)
in (
1.24
).

Proposition 4.1
.

For every
0\leq\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta<\beta_{c}
,

(\beta-\beta^{\prime})(1-E(\beta))\leq\frac{1}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}-\frac{1}{\chi(\beta)}\leq\beta-\beta^{\prime},

(4.2)

and, if
E(\beta)<1
,

\left(\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\right)^{\frac{1+E(\beta)}{1-E(\beta)}}\leq\left(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)}\right)^{2}\leq\left(\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\right)^{1-2E(\beta)}.

(4.3)

In particular, if
E(\beta)\leq\frac{1}{2}
then

\xi(\beta^{\prime})\leq\xi(\beta)
.

By taking
\delta\in(0,1)
and
\beta<\beta(\delta)
, the
second inequality of (
1.25
) is seen to follow
immediately from (
4.3
). By choosing

\beta^{\prime}=0
and
\beta<\beta(\delta)
in (
4.2
), we find that

\beta(1-\delta)\leq 1/\chi(0)=1
,
which implies that

\beta(\delta)\leq(1-\delta)^{-1},

(4.4)

as stated in the first inequality of (
1.25
).
Also, with
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\beta^{\prime}=0
, and
\beta<\beta(\delta)=\beta(\delta)\wedge(1-\delta)^{-1}
,
(
4.2
) rearranges to

\frac{1}{1-\beta(1-\delta)}\leq\chi(\beta)\leq\frac{1}{1-\beta}.

(4.5)

Proof of Proposition
4.1
.

We begin with (
4.2
).
It follows by summation of the differential upper bound (
1.13
) that

\partial_{\beta}\chi(\beta)\leq\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(F_{\beta}*G_{\beta})(x)=\chi(\beta)^{2}.

(4.6)

Similarly, it follows from summation of the differential lower bound (

⁢
I
.2

) that

\partial_{\beta}\chi(\beta)\geq\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(F_{\beta}*[J-H_{\beta}]*G_{\beta})(x)=\left(1-\|H_{\beta}\|_{1}\right)\chi(\beta)^{2}\geq(1-E(\beta))\chi(\beta)^{2}.

(4.7)

The combination of (
4.6
)–(
4.7
) gives

(1-E(\beta))\leq-\partial_{\beta}\chi(\beta)^{-1}\leq 1,

(4.8)

and then integration over the interval
[\beta^{\prime},\beta]
gives (
4.2
).
For later use, we also observe that (
4.6
)–(
4.7
) yield

\partial_{\beta}\log\chi(\beta)\leq\chi(\beta)\leq\frac{1}{1-E(\beta)}\partial_{\beta}\log\chi(\beta).

(4.9)

To begin the proof of (
4.3
), we note that
by definition and by (
1.19
),

\partial_{\beta}(\chi(\beta)\xi(\beta)^{2})=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|_{2}^{2}\partial_{\beta}F_{\beta}(x)\leq\sum_{x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|_{2}^{2}F_{\beta}(y)F_{\beta}(x-y).

(4.10)

We insert
|x|_{2}^{2}=|y|_{2}^{2}+2x\cdot(x-y)+|x-y|_{2}^{2}
in the right-hand side,
and observe that the cross term vanishes by symmetry of
F_{\beta}
.
Therefore,

\partial_{\beta}(\chi(\beta)\xi(\beta)^{2})\leq 2\chi(\beta)^{2}\xi(\beta)^{2}.

(4.11)

We apply the lower bound of
(

⁢
I
.2

) in a similar manner.
With the inequality

\||x|^{2}G_{\beta}\|_{1}=\||x|^{2}F_{\beta}\|_{1}-\sigma_{J}^{2}\chi(\beta)\leq\||x|^{2}F_{\beta}\|_{1},

(4.12)

and with our assumption that
H_{\beta}(x)=H_{\beta}(-x)
, this leads to

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}(\chi(\beta)\xi(\beta)^{2})

\displaystyle\quad\geq\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}|x|_{2}^{2}\left[(F_{\beta}*F_{\beta})(x)-(F_{\beta}*H_{\beta}*G_{\beta})(x)\right]

\displaystyle\quad=2\chi(\beta)^{2}\xi(\beta)^{2}-\chi(\beta)^{2}\||x|_{2}^{2}\cdot H_{\beta}\|_{1}-\chi(\beta)^{2}\|H_{\beta}\|_{1}\xi(\beta)^{2}-\chi(\beta)\|H_{\beta}\|_{1}\cdot\||x|_{2}^{2}\cdot G_{\beta}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\quad\geq 2\chi(\beta)^{2}\xi(\beta)^{2}-\chi(\beta)^{2}\||x|_{2}^{2}\cdot H_{\beta}\|_{1}-2\|H_{\beta}\|_{1}\chi(\beta)^{2}\xi(\beta)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad\geq 2(1-E(\beta))\chi(\beta)^{2}\xi(\beta)^{2}.

(4.13)

Together, (
4.11
) and (
4.13
) give

2(1-E(\beta))\chi(\beta)\leq\partial_{\beta}\log(\chi(\beta)\xi(\beta)^{2})\leq 2\chi(\beta).

(4.14)

With (
4.9
), (
4.14
) gives

2(1-E(\beta))\partial_{\beta}\log\chi(\beta)\leq\partial_{\beta}\log(\chi(\beta)\xi(\beta)^{2})\leq\frac{2}{1-E(\beta)}\partial_{\beta}\log\chi(\beta).

(4.15)

Then, (
4.3
) follows after integration over
[\beta^{\prime},\beta
].
This completes the proof.
∎

The following corollary of Proposition
4.1

shows that the ratios
\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}
and
(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)})^{2}
are comparable up to constants,
as long as
\beta-\beta^{\prime}
is sufficiently small that the
ratios
\frac{1}{4}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}
and

\frac{1}{4}(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)})^{2}
are not smaller than
E(\beta)
.

Corollary 4.2
.

Let
0\leq\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta<\beta_{c}
.
If
E(\beta)\leq\frac{1}{4}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\vee\frac{1}{4}(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)})^{2}
and also
E(\beta)\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
, then

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\leq\left(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)}\right)^{2}\leq 2\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}.

(4.16)

Proof.

The claim follows from (
4.3
) and basic algebra.
Let
a=(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)})^{2}
,
b=\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}
,
and
E=E(\beta)
. Then
0<b\leq 1
, and since
E\leq\tfrac{1}{2}

it follows from Proposition
4.1
that also
0<a\leq 1
. We will use the facts that

t^{t}\geq\frac{1}{2}\;\;\text{and}\;\;t^{t/2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\;\;\text{for $t>0$,}\qquad\frac{1}{1-t}\leq 1+2t\;\;\text{for $t\in[0,\tfrac{1}{2}]$.}

(4.17)

The inequalities in (
4.3
) can be restated as

b\cdot b^{2(\frac{1}{1-E}-1)}\leq a\leq b\cdot\frac{1}{b^{2E}}.

(4.18)

Suppose first that
E\leq\frac{1}{4}b
. Then, a combination of (
4.17
) and (
4.18
) gives
the required bounds

a\geq b\cdot b^{2(\tfrac{1}{1-E}-1)}\geq b\cdot b^{4E}\geq b\cdot b^{b}\geq b\cdot\frac{1}{2}

(4.19)

and

a\leq b\cdot\frac{1}{b^{2E}}\leq b\cdot\frac{1}{b^{b/2}}\leq 2b.

(4.20)

Now suppose that
E\leq\frac{1}{4}a
. In this case we restate (
4.3
) as

a\cdot a^{\frac{1}{1-2E}-1}\leq b\leq a\cdot\frac{1}{a^{\frac{2E}{1+E}}}.

(4.21)

In the lower bound on
b
in
(
4.21
),
the exponent of the second factor is at most
4E\leq a
, so the left-hand side
is bounded below by
\frac{1}{2}a
. The right-hand side of (
4.21
) is
increased if we increase the power to
2E\leq\frac{1}{2}a
, so the right-hand side
is bounded above by
2a
. Together, this gives the desired bounds
\frac{1}{2}b\leq a\leq 2b
in
this case, and completes the proof.
∎

Recall that

Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}=(\beta-\beta^{\prime})\chi(\beta^{\prime}).

(4.22)

In particular,

Z_{0,\beta}=\beta.

(4.23)

The following proposition shows that
Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}
is close to and less than
1

as long as
\beta-\beta^{\prime}
is sufficiently small that the
ratios
\frac{1}{4}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}
and

\frac{1}{8}(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)})^{2}
are not smaller than
E(\beta)
. The upper bound on
Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}
is the useful one in our analysis.

Proposition 4.3

(Bounds on
Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}
)
.

Let
0\leq\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta<\beta_{c}
. Then,

1-\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\leq Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\leq\Big(1-\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\Big)\frac{1}{1-1\wedge E(\beta)}.

(4.24)

In particular, if
E(\beta)\leq\tfrac{1}{4}\tfrac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}
, then

1-\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\leq Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}.

(4.25)

If we assume instead
that
E(\beta)\leq\tfrac{1}{8}(\tfrac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)})^{2}\wedge\tfrac{1}{2}
, then

1-2\left(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)}\right)^{2}\leq Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\leq 1-\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)}\right)^{2}.

(4.26)

Proof.

The upper bound in (
4.2
) implies that

1-\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\leq(\beta-\beta^{\prime})\chi(\beta^{\prime})=Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta},

(4.27)

which is the lower bound in (
4.24
).
The upper bound in (
4.24
) is vacuous if
E(\beta)\geq 1
,
while for
E(\beta)<1
the lower bound in (
4.2
) yields

(1-E(\beta))Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\leq 1-\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)},

(4.28)

which is the upper bound in (
4.24
).

For the upper bound of (
4.25
),
we again use
(1-t)^{-1}\leq 1+2t
for
t\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
,
so that (
4.24
) and the assumption on
E(\beta)
give

Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\leq\left(1-\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}\right)\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}.

(4.29)

Finally, for (
4.26
), we
apply Corollary
4.2
to see that

E(\beta)\leq\frac{1}{8}(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)})^{2}\leq\frac{1}{4}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta)}
, and then apply
(
4.25
) and again use the bounds
of Corollary
4.2
. This completes the proof.
∎

Theorem
1.7
is contained in the following proposition.

Proposition 4.4
.

For
\delta\in(0,1)
,
\beta\leq\beta(\delta)
, and
E=E(\beta(\delta))
, we have

\frac{1}{\chi(\beta(\delta))^{-1}+(\beta(\delta)-\beta)}\leq\chi(\beta)\leq\frac{1}{\chi(\beta(\delta))^{-1}+(\beta(\delta)-\beta)(1-E)},

(4.30)

\chi(\beta)^{1-2E}\leq\frac{\xi(\beta)^{2}}{\sigma_{J}^{2}}\leq\chi(\beta)^{\frac{1+E}{1-E}}.

(4.31)

Assume that
\beta(\delta)=\beta_{c}
and
\chi(\beta_{c})=\infty
. Then,

1\leq\beta_{c}\leq\frac{1}{1-E},

(4.32)

\frac{1}{\beta_{c}-\beta}\leq\chi(\beta)\leq\frac{1}{1-E}\frac{1}{\beta_{c}-\beta},

(4.33)

\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{c}-\beta}\right)^{1-2E}\leq\frac{\xi(\beta)^{2}}{\sigma_{J}^{2}}\leq\left(\frac{1}{(1-E)(\beta_{c}-\beta)}\right)^{\frac{1+E}{1-E}}.

(4.34)

Proof.

The inequality (
4.30
)
follows by replacing
(\beta^{\prime},\beta)
by
(\beta,\beta(\delta))

in (
4.2
),
and (
4.31
) follows from (
4.3
) with
\beta^{\prime}=0
.
To prove (
4.32
), we set
\beta^{\prime}=0
and
take the limit
\beta\uparrow\beta(\delta)=\beta_{c}
in (
4.2
).
The inequalities (
4.33
)–(
4.34
) follow
by setting
\chi(\beta_{c})=\infty
in (
4.30
)–(
4.31
).
∎

4.2
Proof of Proposition
2.2
: bounds on
G_{\beta}
and
F_{\beta}
for
\beta<1

We now prove the bounds on
G_{\beta}
and
F_{\beta}
for
\beta<1

stated in Proposition
2.2
.

Proof of Proposition
2.2
.

Let
\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2}]

and
\beta\leq(1-\delta)\wedge\beta(\delta)
. We start with the bound on
G_{\beta}(x)
. It follows from (
2.8
) and Proposition
2.1
that, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

G_{\beta}(x)\leq\mathbb{C}_{\beta}(x)\leq\delta_{0}(x)+\frac{\mathsf{C}}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2}\exp\left(-\mathsf{c}\sqrt{1-\beta}\frac{|x|}{\xi(0)}\right).

(4.35)

The second inequality in
(
4.3
) gives that

\frac{\xi(\beta)}{\xi(0)}\geq\left(\frac{\chi(\beta)}{\chi(0)}\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}-\delta}.

(4.36)

Combining (
4.5
) with the inequality
1-\delta\geq\beta
yields

\frac{\chi(\beta)}{\chi(0)}\geq\frac{1}{1-(1-\delta)\beta}\geq\frac{1}{1-\beta^{2}}.

(4.37)

Insertion of (
4.37
) into (
4.36
) gives

\displaystyle\sqrt{1-\beta}\cdot\frac{\xi(\beta)}{\xi(0)}

\displaystyle\geq(1-\beta)^{\delta}\left(\frac{1-\beta}{1-\beta^{2}}\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}-\delta}

\displaystyle\geq\delta^{\delta}\left(\frac{1}{1+\beta}\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}-\delta}\geq\delta^{\delta}\left(\frac{1}{2-\delta}\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}-\delta}\geq\frac{1}{2},

(4.38)

where last inequality follows from elementary calculus to
minimise the penultimate expression. Combining (
4.35
), and (
4.38
),
we obtain, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

G_{\beta}(x)\leq\mathbb{C}_{\beta}(x)\leq\delta_{0}(x)+\frac{\mathsf{C}}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2}\exp\left(-\frac{\mathsf{c}}{2}\frac{|x|}{\xi(\beta)}\right).

(4.39)

This completes the proof of (
2.9
).

Next we consider
F_{\beta}=J*G_{\beta}
. By (
2.8
),

F_{\beta}(x)\leq(J*\mathbb{C}_{\beta})(x)=\frac{1}{\beta}(\mathbb{C}_{\beta}(x)-\delta_{0}(x)).

(4.40)

For the case
\beta\geq\frac{1}{2}
, we immediately obtain (
2.10
)
from the bound on
\mathbb{C}_{\beta}(x)
in (
4.39
),
with
\mathbf{C}_{0}=2\mathsf{C}
.
For the remaining case
\beta<\frac{1}{2}
, it follows from
the monotonicity of
F_{\beta}
, (
4.40
), and
the bound on
\mathbb{C}_{1/2}(x)
from
(
2.6
) that

\displaystyle F_{\beta}(x)\leq F_{1/2}(x)

\displaystyle\leq 2(\mathbb{C}_{1/2}(x)-\delta_{0}(x))

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{\mathsf{C}}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2}\exp\Big(-\mathsf{c}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\Big).

(4.41)

Since
\xi(\beta)\geq\xi(0)=\sigma
by (
4.36
),
we then obtain (
2.10
) with
\mathbf{C}_{0}=2\mathsf{C}
and

\mathbf{c}_{0}=\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{c}
, from the observation that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{1}{\xi(0)}\geq\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{\xi(\beta)}.

(4.42)

This concludes the proof.
∎

4.3
Regularity of the effective random walk

We now prove Proposition
2.8
, which states
that the effective random walk introduced in
Definition
2.3
is uniformly regular for
\beta<\beta(\delta_{\textup{reg}})
with
\delta_{\textup{reg}}
sufficiently small. Consequently, for every
\beta<\beta(\delta_{\textup{reg}})
,
the effective random walk
obeys the anti-concentration and Green function estimates of Theorem
2.10
, with uniform constants
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}},\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}})
that depend only on
d
.
The fact that
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},\mathsf{C}_{\textup{reg}})
and

(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}},\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}})
do not depend on
J
or
\beta
is essential for our proof.

Proof of Proposition
2.8
.

The effective random walk
X
with law
\mathbb{P}_{\beta}
is symmetric by definition.
Let

L:=2^{7}=128.

(4.43)

Let
\delta>0
, to be chosen small enough. In particular, we assume that

\delta\leq\frac{1}{4L}
.
Fix
\beta<\beta(\delta)
. Then
\chi(\beta)<\infty
.
Recall that

M_{\beta}(s)=\mathbb{E}_{\beta}[\exp(s(\mathbf{e}_{1}\cdot X_{1})/\xi(\beta))].

(4.44)

It suffices to prove that we can choose
\delta,c_{1}>0
such
that
M_{\beta}(c_{1})\leq 3
for every
\beta<\beta(\delta)
.

We define a finite integer
K
by

K:=\min\Big\{k\geq 0:\text{there exists $\beta_{0}<(1-\textstyle\frac{1}{2L})$
such that $\chi(\beta_{0})=L^{-k}\chi(\beta)$}\Big\}.

(4.45)

With
\beta_{0}
so defined, for
0\leq k\leq K
we introduce
\beta_{k}
by setting

\chi(\beta_{k})=L^{k}\chi(\beta_{0}).

(4.46)

In particular,
\beta_{K}=\beta
.
We will prove by induction on
k
that
M_{\beta_{k}}(c_{1})\leq 3
,
for appropriate
\delta,c_{1}>0
.

To start the induction, we prove that
M_{\beta_{0}}(c_{1})\leq 3
,
with
c_{1}>0
to be chosen.
By Proposition
4.1
and the fact that
(by definition of
\beta(\delta)
)

E(\beta)\leq\delta\leq\frac{1}{4L}\leq\frac{1}{2},

(4.47)

we have
\xi(\beta_{0})\geq\xi(0)=\sigma_{J}
.
Let
\lambda_{0}=\exp[c_{1}/c_{0}]
where
c_{0}
is given by (
1.10
).
Then
M_{0}(c_{1}\sigma_{J}/\xi(\beta_{0}))\leq\exp(c_{1}\tfrac{\sigma_{J}}{\xi(\beta_{0})}\tfrac{R_{J}}{\sigma_{J}})\leq\lambda_{0}
.
We require that
c_{1}=c_{1}(c_{0},L,d)>0
be sufficiently small that

\lambda_{0}\leq 1+\tfrac{1}{4L}
.
Then
\beta_{0}\lambda_{0}<(1-\tfrac{1}{2L})(1+\tfrac{1}{4L})<1

and hence, by Corollary
2.9
,

M_{\beta_{0}}(c_{1})\leq\frac{1}{\chi(\beta_{0})}\frac{M_{0}(c_{1}\sigma_{J}/\xi(\beta_{0}))}{1-\beta_{0}M_{0}(c_{1}\sigma_{J}/\xi(\beta_{0}))}\leq\frac{\lambda_{0}}{\chi(\beta_{0})}\frac{1}{1-\beta_{0}\lambda_{0}}.

(4.48)

Also, since
\delta\leq\frac{1}{4L}
, (
4.5
) implies that

\frac{1}{\chi(\beta_{0})}=\frac{\chi(0)}{\chi(\beta_{0})}\leq 1-\beta_{0}(1-\delta)\leq 1-\beta_{0}\left(1-\frac{1}{4L}\right).

(4.49)

As a result, we have

\displaystyle M_{\beta_{0}}(c_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\left(1+\frac{1}{4L}\right)\max_{t\in[0,1-\tfrac{1}{2L}]}\frac{1-(1-\tfrac{1}{4L})t}{1-(1+\tfrac{1}{4L})t}

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{1}{4L}\right)\frac{1-(1-\tfrac{1}{4L})(1-\tfrac{1}{2L})}{1-(1+\tfrac{1}{4L})(1-\tfrac{1}{2L})}\leq 3.

(4.50)

To advance the induction, we assume that
M_{\beta_{k}}(c_{1})\leq C_{1}

and prove that the same bound holds when
k
is replaced by
k+1
.
To abbreviate the notation, we write

Z_{k}=Z_{\beta_{k},\beta_{k+1}}.

(4.51)

Let

r_{k}:=\frac{\xi(\beta_{k})}{\xi(\beta_{k+1})}.

(4.52)

By Corollary
2.9
, if
Z_{k}M_{\beta_{k}}(c_{1}r_{k})<1
then

M_{\beta_{k+1}}(c_{1})\leq\frac{\chi(\beta_{k})}{\chi(\beta_{k+1})}\frac{M_{\beta_{k}}(c_{1}r_{k})}{1-Z_{k}M_{\beta_{k}}(c_{1}r_{k})}.

(4.53)

To apply this, we must show that
Z_{k}M_{\beta_{k}}(c_{1}r_{k})<1
.
For this, we will make use of the fact that (
4.47
),
Corollary
4.2
and Proposition
4.3

imply the upper bounds

\displaystyle r_{k}^{2}\leq 2\frac{\chi(\beta_{k})}{\chi(\beta_{k+1})}=\frac{2}{L},\qquad Z_{k}

\displaystyle\leq 1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\chi(\beta_{k})}{\chi(\beta_{k+1})}=1-\frac{1}{2L}.

(4.54)

It is elementary that for any
0\leq|u|\leq|v|
, we have

\cosh u\leq 1+\frac{u^{2}}{2}+\frac{u^{4}}{v^{4}}\cosh v,

(4.55)

and hence for any real and symmetric random variable
U
and
any
0\leq s\leq t
,

\mathbb{E}[\exp(sU)]=\mathbb{E}[\cosh sU]\leq 1+\frac{s^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}[U^{2}]+\left(\frac{s}{t}\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}[\exp(tU)].

(4.56)

We apply (
4.56
)
to
\mathbb{E}_{\beta_{k}}
with
s=c_{1}r_{k}
,

t=c_{1}
, and
U=(\mathbf{e}_{1}\cdot X_{1})/\xi(\beta_{k})
.
By symmetry,
\mathbb{E}_{\beta_{k}}[U^{2}]=\frac{1}{d}
.
Therefore, by (
4.54
)
and the induction hypothesis,

\displaystyle M_{\beta_{k}}(c_{1}r_{k})

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{c_{1}^{2}}{2d}r_{k}^{2}+3r_{k}^{4}\leq 1+\frac{c_{1}^{2}}{2d}\frac{2}{L}+3\left(\frac{2}{L}\right)^{2}\leq 1+\left(c_{1}^{2}+\frac{12}{L}\right)\frac{1}{L}.

(4.57)

We choose
c_{1}
small depending on
L
, so that the above gives

\displaystyle M_{\beta_{k}}(c_{1}r_{k})\leq 1+\frac{15}{L}\frac{1}{L}.

(4.58)

Then, by (
4.54
), and since
\tfrac{15}{L}=\tfrac{15}{128}<1/2
,

Z_{k}\,M_{\beta_{k}}(c_{1}r_{k})\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{2L}\right)\left(1+\frac{15}{L}\frac{1}{L}\right)\leq 1-\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{15}{L}\right)\frac{1}{L}<1.

(4.59)

This allows us to apply (
4.53
), to obtain

\displaystyle M_{\beta_{k+1}}(c_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{L}\frac{1+\frac{15}{L}\frac{1}{L}}{(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{15}{L})\frac{1}{L}}=\frac{2+\frac{30}{L^{2}}}{1-\frac{30}{L}}.

(4.60)

By choice of
L
, the right-hand side is less than

3
.
This concludes the induction and the proof.

Something important just happened:
we gain by
Z\leq 1-\frac{1}{2L}
and we lose by
1+\frac{15}{L^{2}}
in (
4.57
)
when
c_{1}
is small
enough.
When we take the product of these two effects, we still gain.
The potentially bad effect of the constant
15
is overcome by a choice of large
L
.

To summarise the two cases, we have obtained that for
L=128
and
\delta\leq\tfrac{1}{4L}
, for every
\beta<\beta(\delta)
we have

M_{\beta}(c_{1})\leq 3.

(4.61)

We now set
\delta_{\textup{reg}}:=\frac{1}{4L}=2^{-9}
and
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},\mathsf{C}_{\textup{reg}})=(c_{1},3)
.
This completes the proof.
∎

4.4
Stability of the finite-volume susceptibility

We now prove the stability estimate stated in Proposition
2.12
and reformulated in (
2.32
). For
k\geq 0
, the finite-volume susceptibility is defined by

\chi_{k}(\beta)=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{k}}F_{\beta}(x).

(4.62)

Proposition 4.5

(Stability estimate)
.

Let
d>2
. Suppose that
J
and
G
both obey Assumption
I
.
There exist
\delta_{\textup{stab}}\in(0,\tfrac{1}{10}]
and
C_{\textup{stab}}>0
, depending only on
d
, such that, for every
\beta<\beta(\delta_{\textup{stab}})
,

\chi_{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}(\beta)\leq C_{\textup{stab}}\chi(\beta^{\prime}),\qquad\forall\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta.

(4.63)

Remark 4.6
.

Let
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},\mathsf{C}_{\textup{reg}}=3)
be the regularity constants of the effective random walk from Proposition
2.8
, and let
\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}=\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},\mathsf{C}_{\textup{reg}})>0
be the constants
of Theorem
2.10
. The proof of
Proposition
4.5
shows that the constant
C_{\textup{stab}}
can
be chosen as
C_{\textup{stab}}=32\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}
.

The proof of Proposition
4.5
builds on the following
three
elementary lemmas. The first lemma is a straightforward extension of Lemma
2.5
. Its bound on
F_{\beta}(x)
serves as the starting point for
bounding
\chi_{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}(\beta)
in the proof of Proposition
4.5
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
0\leq\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta<\beta_{c}
. For every
T\geq 1
and every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

F_{\beta}(x)\leq(Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\vee 1)^{T}\Big(\chi(\beta^{\prime})\big(\mathbb{G}_{1,\beta^{\prime}}(x)-\delta_{0}(x)\big)+\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(z)\mathbb{P}_{\beta^{\prime}}[X_{T}=x-z]\Big).

(4.64)

Proof.

Let
T\geq 1
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Recall from (
2.15
) (with
G
replaced by
F
) that

F_{\beta}(x)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{T-1}Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}^{k}\mathbb{E}_{\beta^{\prime}}[F_{\beta^{\prime}}(x-X_{k})]+Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\beta^{\prime}}[F_{\beta}(x-X_{T})].

(4.65)

By (
4.65
), and since
Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\leq(Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\vee 1)
, we obtain

\displaystyle F_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\leq(Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\vee 1)^{T-1}\chi(\beta^{\prime})\sum_{k=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{P}_{\beta^{\prime}}[X_{k+1}=x]+(Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\vee 1)^{T}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathbb{P}_{\beta^{\prime}}[X_{T}=z]F_{\beta}(x-z)

\displaystyle\leq(Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\vee 1)\Big(\chi(\beta^{\prime})\big(\mathbb{G}_{1,\beta^{\prime}}(x)-\delta_{0}(x)\big)+\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(z)\mathbb{P}_{\beta^{\prime}}[X_{T}=x-z]\Big).

(4.66)

This completes the proof.
∎

The second lemma provides a means, given a bound on
a finite-volume susceptibility for one volume, to extract a bound for a larger volume. As is clear from
its proof,
the exponent
\frac{5}{2}
in (
4.68
) could be reduced to any exponent

a>2
by choosing
\delta
appropriately small.
We use
\frac{5}{2}
as a concrete choice; later we only need that
d>a
,
which is satisfied for every
d>2
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\delta\leq\frac{1}{10}
and
0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta<\beta(\delta)
. Assume that there exists
C_{1}\geq 1
such that, for every
\beta^{\prime\prime}<\beta
with
\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}\geq 2
, the inequality

\chi_{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}(\beta)\leq C_{1}\chi(\beta^{\prime\prime})

(4.67)

holds. Then, for every
k\geq 2
,

\chi_{k\xi(\beta^{\prime})}(\beta)\leq C_{1}k^{5/2}\chi(\beta^{\prime}).

(4.68)

Proof.

Fix
\delta\leq\frac{1}{10}
and
0<\beta^{\prime}<\beta<\beta(\delta)
. Let
k\geq 2
. We consider two cases. First, suppose that
k\xi(\beta^{\prime})\geq\xi(\beta)
. By (
4.3
) and the fact that
\tfrac{2}{1-2\delta}\leq\tfrac{5}{2}
,

\chi_{k\xi(\beta^{\prime})}(\beta)\leq\chi(\beta)=\frac{\chi(\beta)}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\chi(\beta^{\prime})\leq\left(\frac{\xi(\beta)}{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}\right)^{5/2}\chi(\beta^{\prime})\leq k^{5/2}\chi(\beta^{\prime}).

(4.69)

If instead
k\xi(\beta^{\prime})<\xi(\beta)
, then we define
\beta^{\prime\prime}<\beta
by the
requirement that
\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})=k\xi(\beta^{\prime})
.
Since
k\geq 2
, we have
\tfrac{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}\geq 2
.
It then follows from (
4.67
) that

\chi_{k\xi(\beta^{\prime})}(\beta)=\chi_{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}(\beta)\leq C_{1}\chi(\beta^{\prime\prime})\leq C_{1}\left(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}\right)^{5/2}\chi(\beta^{\prime})=C_{1}k^{5/2}\chi(\beta^{\prime}).

(4.70)

This concludes the proof.
∎

The third and final lemma is an elementary estimate.
It will be used in conjunction with Lemma
4.8

to handle the convolution appearing in the
last term of (
4.64
).

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
\alpha,K>0
and
\xi\geq 1
. Suppose
that
f:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
satisfies, for every
m\geq 2
,

\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{m\xi}}f(y)\leq Km^{\alpha}.

(4.71)

Then, for every
\kappa>0
,
there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(\kappa,\alpha)>0
such that, for every

T\geq 4
,

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(x)\exp\left(-\kappa\frac{|x|}{\xi\sqrt{T}}\right)\leq C_{0}KT^{\alpha/2}.

(4.72)

Proof.

We decompose the sum into annuli centred at the origin.
For this, we define radii
u_{0}:=0
,
u_{1}:=\xi\sqrt{T}
, and
u_{k+1}:=2u_{k}

for
k\geq 1
. We also let
\varphi_{T}(x)=\exp(-\kappa\frac{|x|}{\xi\sqrt{T}})
. Then, as
\sqrt{T}\geq 2
, we can apply our hypothesis on
f

to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}f(x)\varphi_{T}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 0}\sum_{u_{k}\leq|x|<u_{k+1}}f(x)\varphi_{T}(x)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{\xi\sqrt{T}}}f(x)+\sum_{m\geq 1}e^{-\kappa 2^{m-1}}\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{2^{m}\xi\sqrt{T}}}f(x)

\displaystyle\leq KT^{\alpha/2}+\sum_{m\geq 1}e^{-\kappa 2^{m-1}}K(2^{m}\sqrt{T})^{\alpha}.

(4.73)

The right-hand side is bounded by a constant multiple of
KT^{\alpha/2}
,
with a constant depending only on
\kappa
and
\alpha
.
∎

With Lemmas
4.7
–
4.9
, we are now in a position to prove Proposition
4.5
.

Proof of Proposition
4.5
.

Let
\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{10}]
to be chosen small enough, and
C_{\textup{stab}}
to be chosen large enough. Fix
\beta<\beta(\delta)
. We recursively define a decreasing sequence
(\beta_{\ell})_{\ell\geq 0}
as follows:

\bullet

Set
\beta_{0}:=\beta
.

\bullet

Assume that
\beta_{0},\ldots,\beta_{\ell}
have been constructed. If
\xi(\beta_{\ell})\in[\xi(0),2\xi(0))
, we stop the construction. Otherwise, we define
\beta_{\ell+1}\in[0,\beta_{\ell})
by
\xi(\beta_{\ell+1})=\tfrac{1}{2}\xi(\beta_{\ell})
.

\bullet

Let
M\geq 0
be the largest
\ell
such that
\beta_{\ell}
has been constructed, and let
\beta_{M+1}=0
.

We now assume that
\delta\leq\delta_{\textup{reg}}
, with
\delta_{\textup{reg}}
given by Proposition
2.8
. For such
\delta
, the effective random walk is uniformly
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},3)
-regular for some
\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}}=\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}}(d)>0
. The bounds for regular random walks in Theorem
2.10
therefore apply.
Let
\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\geq 1
be given by Theorem
2.10
for the pair
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},3)
.

We will prove that if
\delta
is small enough, then for every
0\leq\ell\leq M
, we have

\chi_{\xi(\beta_{\ell})}(\beta)\leq 4\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\chi(\beta_{\ell}).

(4.74)

We claim that the above is sufficient to conclude the proof, with
C_{\textup{stab}}:=32\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}
. Indeed, let
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
(there is nothing
to prove if
\beta^{\prime}=\beta
).
There exists a unique
0\leq\ell\leq M
such that
\beta^{\prime}\in[\beta_{\ell+1},\beta_{\ell})
.
Therefore,

\chi_{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}(\beta)\leq\chi_{\xi(\beta_{\ell})}(\beta)\leq 4\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\chi(\beta_{\ell})=4\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\frac{\chi(\beta_{\ell})}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\chi(\beta^{\prime})\leq 32\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\chi(\beta^{\prime}),

(4.75)

where in the first inequality we used
\delta\leq\tfrac{1}{2}
and
Proposition
4.1

to see that
\xi(\beta^{\prime})\leq\xi(\beta_{\ell})
,
in the second we used (
4.74
),
and in the last inequality we used (
4.3
) and the assumption that
\delta\leq\tfrac{1}{10}
to obtain

\frac{\chi(\beta_{\ell})}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\leq\left(\frac{\xi(\beta_{\ell})}{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}\right)^{\tfrac{2}{1-2\delta}}\leq 2^{3}=8.

(4.76)

We prove (
4.74
) by induction on
\ell
. First, observe that (
4.74
) for
\ell=0
follows from the
fact that
4\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\geq 4\geq 1
.

To advance the induction, we fix
1\leq\ell\leq M
and assume that (
4.74
) holds for all values
m\leq\ell-1
. Our goal is to prove (
4.74
) at
\ell
. Let
\beta^{\prime}=\beta_{\ell}
,
n:=\xi(\beta^{\prime})
,
Z:=Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}
, and
\mathbb{P}=\mathbb{P}_{\beta^{\prime}}
. By (
4.64
), for every
T\geq 1
, and every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

F_{\beta}(x)\leq(Z\vee 1)^{T}\Big(\chi(\beta^{\prime})\mathbb{G}(x)+\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(z)\mathbb{P}[X_{T}=x-z]\Big),

(4.77)

where
\mathbb{G}
is the Green function associated with the effective random walk at
\beta^{\prime}
at
Z=1
.
Summation of (
4.77
) over
x\in\Lambda_{n}
gives

\chi_{n}(\beta)\leq(Z\vee 1)^{T}\bigg(\mathbb{G}(\Lambda_{n})\chi(\beta^{\prime})+\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(z)\mathbb{P}[X_{T}\in\Lambda_{n}(z)]\bigg).

(4.78)

Since it follows from Theorem
2.10
that

\mathbb{G}(\Lambda_{n})\leq\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}
, the first term on the right-hand side
of (
4.78
) obeys

(Z\vee 1)^{T}\mathbb{G}(\Lambda_{n})\chi(\beta^{\prime})\leq(Z\vee 1)^{T}\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\chi(\beta^{\prime}).

(4.79)

For the last term in (
4.78
), the anti-concentration estimate (
2.29
) gives, for every
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

\mathbb{P}[X_{T}\in\Lambda_{n}(z)]\leq\frac{\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}}{T^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}}\frac{|z|}{n\sqrt{T}}\right).

(4.80)

In order to apply Lemma
4.9
,
we need to show that there exists
C_{2}=C_{2}(d)>0
such that, for every
k\geq 2
,

\sum_{x\in\Lambda_{kn}}F_{\beta}(x)=\chi_{kn}(\beta)\leq C_{2}\chi(\beta^{\prime})k^{5/2}.

(4.81)

By Lemma
4.8
, (
4.81
) will follow if we show the existence of
C_{3}=C_{3}(d)>0
such that for any
\beta^{\prime\prime}<\beta
with
\tfrac{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}\geq 2
,

\chi_{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}(\beta)\leq C_{3}\chi(\beta^{\prime\prime}).

(4.82)

However, if
\beta^{\prime\prime}
is as above, then
\beta^{\prime\prime}\geq\beta_{\ell-1}
. Therefore, by the induction hypothesis and the same computation as in (
4.75
), (
4.82
) is satisfied with
C_{3}=32\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}
. Hence, the conclusion of Lemma
4.8
holds and so does (
4.81
) for some
C_{2}=C_{2}(d)>0
. We can now take
T\geq 4
and apply Lemma
4.9
with
\alpha=\frac{5}{2}
and
K=C_{2}\chi(\beta^{\prime})
to find
C_{4}=C_{4}(d)>0
such that

\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(x)\varphi_{T}(x)\leq C_{4}\chi(\beta^{\prime})T^{5/4}.

(4.83)

The combination of (
4.80
), (
4.82
) and (
4.83
) gives the existence of
C_{5}=C_{5}(d)>0
such that

\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(z)\mathbb{P}[X_{T}\in\Lambda_{n}(z)]\leq\frac{C_{5}}{T^{\frac{1}{2}(d-\frac{5}{2})}}\chi(\beta^{\prime}).

(4.84)

Since
d\geq 3
,
we may choose
T
large enough (depending only on
d
) that

\frac{C_{5}}{T^{\frac{1}{2}(d-\frac{5}{2})}}\leq\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}.

(4.85)

This shows that

(Z\vee 1)^{T}\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(z)\mathbb{P}[X_{T}\in\Lambda_{n}(z)]\leq(Z\vee 1)^{T}\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\chi(\beta^{\prime}).

(4.86)

We now insert the bounds (
4.79
) and (
4.86
)
into (
4.78
), and obtain

\chi_{n}(\beta)\leq 2(Z\vee 1)^{T}\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\chi(\beta^{\prime}).

(4.87)

Finally, it follows from (
4.24
) that
(Z\vee 1)\leq(1-\delta)^{-1}
. We choose
\delta

small enough (depending on
d
) such that
(1-\delta)^{-T}\leq 2
. For this choice, (
4.87
) gives
\chi_{n}(\beta)\leq 4\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}\chi(\beta^{\prime})
, and the induction step is complete. This concludes the proof.
∎

5
Proof of the multiscale bounds

We now turn to the proofs of the three remaining propositions,
Propositions
3.5
–
3.7
. Throughout the section, we fix
d>2
and
\varepsilon>0
. We also suppose that
J,G
satisfy Assumption
I
. For convenience, we drop the subscript
J
in the notations and write
simply
\sigma
instead of
\sigma_{J}
. We always assume that
\delta\leq\delta_{\textup{reg}}\wedge\delta_{\textup{stab}}
so that Propositions
2.8
and
4.5
hold. As a result, by Proposition
2.8
, we know that the effective random walk is uniformly
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},3)
-regular for every
\beta<\beta(\delta)
. This allows us to
apply
Theorem
2.10
with constants
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}},\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}})
which depend on
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},\mathsf{C}_{\textup{reg}}=3,d)
.

5.1
Proof of Proposition
3.5
: bound on
F_{\beta}(x)
for
|x|\leq 2\sigma

Recall that the constant
\mathsf{C}
, which appears in (
3.15
),
is the constant arising in the estimate for the Green function
\mathbb{C}
of the random walk
\mathbb{P}_{J}
,
in Proposition
2.1
.

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

We will prove that there exists
\delta_{s}\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
such that, for every
\beta<\beta(\delta_{s})
and every
|x|\leq 2\xi(0)
,

F_{\beta}(x)\leq 2\big(\mathbb{C}_{1}(x)-\delta_{0}(x)\big)+2\mathsf{C}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma}\right)^{d}.

(5.1)

We can bound the right-hand side using
Proposition
2.1
, which together with
|x|\leq 2\sigma
gives

\big(\mathbb{C}_{1}(x)-\delta_{0}(x)\big)+\mathsf{C}\left(\frac{1}{2\sigma}\right)^{d}\leq\frac{\mathsf{C}}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2}+\frac{\mathsf{C}}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2}.

(5.2)

With (
5.1
), this proves our goal
(
3.15
).

It remains to prove (
5.1
). Let
\delta\in(0,\tfrac{1}{2}]
to be taken small enough, let
\beta<\beta(\delta)
, and set
\beta^{\prime}=0
.
Then
\chi(\beta^{\prime})=1
,
\mathbb{P}_{\beta^{\prime}}=\mathbb{P}_{J}
, and
\mathbb{G}_{1,\beta^{\prime}}=\mathbb{C}_{1}
.
We write
Z:=Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}
. By (
4.64
), for every
T\geq 1
and every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we have

F_{\beta}(x)\leq(Z\vee 1)^{T}\Big(\big(\mathbb{C}_{1}(x)-\delta_{0}(x)\big)+\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(z)\mathbb{P}_{J}[X_{T}=x-z]\Big).

(5.3)

Our main effort is to bound the second term on the right-hand side of (
5.3
). We argue as in the proof of Proposition
4.5
.
The anti-concentration estimate (
2.5
) asserts that there exists
(\mathsf{c},\mathsf{C})
(depending on
d
and
c_{0}
from (
1.10
))
such that, for every
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\mathbb{P}_{J}[X_{T}=z]\leq\frac{\mathsf{C}}{\sigma^{d}T^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\mathsf{c}\frac{|z|}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\right).

(5.4)

Since
|x|\leq 2\xi(0)
, the choice
T\geq 4
in (
5.4
) gives, for every
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{J}[X_{T}=x-z]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{C}}{\sigma^{d}T^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\mathsf{c}\frac{|z|}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\right)\exp\left(\mathsf{c}\frac{2}{\sqrt{T}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{C}e^{\mathsf{c}}}{\sigma^{d}T^{d/2}}\exp\left(-\mathsf{c}\frac{|z|}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\right).

(5.5)

Proposition
4.5
and Lemma
4.8
then
imply that, if
\delta\leq\delta_{\textup{stab}}
, then for every
k\geq 2
,

\sum_{z\in\Lambda_{k\sigma}}F_{\beta}(z)\leq C_{\textup{stab}}k^{5/2}\chi(\beta^{\prime})=C_{\textup{stab}}k^{5/2}.

(5.6)

We apply Lemma
4.9

with
\alpha=\frac{5}{2}
,
f=F_{\beta}
, and
K=C_{\textup{stab}}
, to obtain
C_{1}=C_{1}(\mathsf{c},d)>0
such that

\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(z)\exp\left(-\mathsf{c}\frac{|z|}{\sigma\sqrt{T}}\right)\leq C_{1}T^{5/4}.

(5.7)

The combination of (
5.5
) and (
5.7
) gives
C_{2}=C_{2}(\mathsf{c},d)>0
such that, for every
T\geq 4
and every
|x|\leq 2\xi(0)
,

\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta}(z)\mathbb{P}_{J}[X_{T}=x-z]\leq\frac{C_{2}}{\sigma^{d}T^{\frac{1}{2}(d-\frac{5}{2})}}.

(5.8)

Since
d>2
, we may now choose
T
large enough so that

\frac{C_{2}}{T^{\frac{1}{2}(d-\frac{5}{2})}}\leq\frac{\mathsf{C}}{2^{d}}.

(5.9)

Finally, we pick
\delta
sufficiently small so that the prefactor on the right-hand side of (
5.3
) satisfies
(Z\vee 1)^{T}\leq(1-\delta)^{-T}\leq 2
. With (
5.7
)–(
5.8
), we see that the second term on the right-hand side of (
5.3
)
is at most
2\mathsf{C}(2\sigma)^{-d}
. This proves (
5.1
) and completes the proof.
∎

5.2
Proof of Proposition
3.6
: contraction step for
typical scales

Sections
5.2
and
5.3
are dedicated to the proof of the
contraction step of the bootstrap argument, namely
Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
.
We now prove Proposition
3.6
.

Recall that, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, for every
\beta\geq 0
, and for
\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C}>0
,

\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C};x)
and
\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C})

are the statements:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C};x):

\displaystyle\quad F_{\beta}(x)\leq\frac{\mathbf{C}}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2-\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\mathbf{c}\frac{|x|}{\xi(\beta)}\right),

(5.10)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C}):

\displaystyle\quad\text{$\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C};x)$ holds for all $x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}$}.

(5.11)

Proposition
3.6
can be reformulated as follows: if
\eta>0
,
\mathbf{C}\geq 16\mathsf{C}
,
\mathbf{c}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathsf{c}\wedge\tfrac{1}{4}\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}}\wedge 1
, then there exists
\delta_{2}
small enough such that for every
\beta<\beta(\delta_{2})
, if
\mathcal{H}_{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C})
holds for
\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta
, then
\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\tfrac{\mathbf{C}}{8};x)
holds for every
|x|\geq\eta\xi(\beta)\vee 2\xi(0)
.
The improved bound will be obtained by relying on the finite-difference inequality (

⁢
I
.1

).

We stress that the proof of Proposition
3.6
is the only place in the entire paper where the assumption that
\varepsilon>0
is used.
We begin by stating two simple lemmas. The first one, an elementary consequence of (

⁢
I
.1

), is the starting point of the proof. Recall from (
2.12
) that the Green function of the effective random
walk is defined, for
Z\in[0,1]
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, by

\mathbb{G}_{Z,\beta^{\prime}}(x)=\sum_{k\geq 0}Z^{k}\mathbb{P}_{\beta^{\prime}}[X_{k}=x].

(5.12)

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
0\leq\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta<\beta_{c}
be such that
Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}<1
. For every
m>0
and every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
|x|>m
, we have

\displaystyle F_{\beta}(x)\leq\frac{\chi_{m}(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\sup_{y\in\Lambda_{m}(0)}F_{\beta}(x-y)+\sum_{y\notin\Lambda_{m}(0)}F_{\beta^{\prime}}(y)\mathbb{G}_{Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta},\beta^{\prime}}(x-y).

(5.13)

Proof.

By the finite-difference upper bound in (

⁢
I
.1

),

\displaystyle F_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\leq F_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)+(\beta-\beta^{\prime})\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}F_{\beta^{\prime}}(y)F_{\beta}(x-y).

(5.14)

The contribution to the convolution sum due to
y\in\Lambda_{m}(0)

can be rewritten and bounded (since
Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}<1
) by

\frac{Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{m}(0)}F_{\beta^{\prime}}(y)F_{\beta}(x-y)\leq\frac{\chi_{m}(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\sup_{y\in\Lambda_{m}(0)}F_{\beta}(x-y).

(5.15)

This gives the first term on the right-hand side
of (
5.13
).

From (
2.16
) with
G
replaced by
F
, we see that

\displaystyle(\beta-\beta^{\prime})F_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\leq Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\sum_{k\geq 0}Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}^{k}\mathbb{P}_{\beta^{\prime}}[X_{k+1}=x]=\mathbb{G}_{Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta},\beta^{\prime}}(x)-\delta_{0}(x).

(5.16)

By (
5.16
), the remaining part of the right-hand side
of (
5.14
) is therefore at most

\displaystyle F_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)+\sum_{y\notin\Lambda_{m}(0)}F_{\beta^{\prime}}(y)\big[\mathbb{G}_{Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta},\beta^{\prime}}(x-y)-\delta_{0}(x-y)\big].

(5.17)

By our assumption that
|x|>m
, the first term is cancelled by the Kronecker delta.
This completes the proof.
∎

To bound the last term on the right-hand side of
(
5.13
), we will use the following convolution lemma. We defer the elementary proof to Appendix
B
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
a,b,c_{1},c_{2},\sigma,\xi>0
,

\mu>0
and
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
.
Suppose that the functions
f,g:\mathbb{Z}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
satisfy

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\frac{1}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2-\varepsilon}e^{-a|x|/\xi},

(5.18)

\displaystyle g(\Lambda_{\xi}(x))

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\left(\frac{\xi}{\xi\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2}e^{-b|x|/\xi}.

(5.19)

Then, there exists
C_{a,\mu}>0
(depending on
a,\mu,d
) such that for every
|x|\geq 2(\sigma\vee\xi)
,

\sum_{y\notin\Lambda_{\mu\xi}(0)}f(y)g(x-y)\leq\frac{c_{1}}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\left(2^{d}\|g\|_{1}e^{-a|x|/2\xi}+c_{2}C_{a,\mu}\left(\frac{\xi}{|x|}\right)^{\varepsilon}e^{-b|x|/2\xi}\right).

(5.20)

We are now in a position to prove Proposition
3.6
. Let
\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}}
and
\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}
be given by Theorem
2.10
, for the pair
(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},\mathsf{C}_{\textup{reg}})=(\mathsf{c}_{\textup{reg}},3)
provided by Proposition
2.8
.

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

Let
d>2
,
\varepsilon>0
and
\eta>0
.
Let
\mathbf{C}\geq 16\mathsf{C}
and

\mathbf{c}\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathsf{c}\wedge\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}}\wedge 1
.
Our goal is to prove that there exists
\delta_{2}=\delta_{2}(\eta,\varepsilon,d)\leq\delta_{s}\wedge\delta_{\textup{reg}}\wedge\tfrac{1}{2}
such that, for every
\beta<\beta(\delta_{2})
,
if
\mathcal{H}_{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C})
holds for every
\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta
,
then, for every
|x|\geq\eta\xi(\beta)\vee 2\xi(0)
,

F_{\beta}(x)\leq\frac{\mathbf{C}}{8}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}\exp\left(-\mathbf{c}\frac{|x|}{\xi(\beta)}\right).

(5.21)

On the right-hand side, we used our assumption that

|x|\geq 2\xi(0)=2\sigma
.

Let
\delta\leq\tfrac{1}{4}\wedge\delta_{\textup{reg}}\wedge\delta_{\textup{stab}}
, where
\delta_{\textup{reg}}
and
\delta_{\textup{stab}}
are respectively given by
Propositions
2.8
and
4.5
.
If
\beta\leq\beta_{0}:=(1-\delta)\wedge\beta(\delta)
, the
desired bound (
5.21
) is already known from (
2.10
).
We can therefore assume that
\beta\in(\beta_{0},\beta(\delta))
.
Fix such a
\beta
.
We wish to apply Lemma
5.1
, which states that
if
0\leq\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta
are such that
Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}<1
,
and if
|x|>m>0
, then

\displaystyle F_{\beta}(x)\leq\frac{\chi_{m}(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\sup_{y\in\Lambda_{m}(0)}F_{\beta}(x-y)+\sum_{y\notin\Lambda_{m}(0)}F_{\beta^{\prime}}(y)\mathbb{G}_{Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta},\beta^{\prime}}(x-y).

(5.22)

Later in the proof, we write the two terms on the right-hand side of (
5.22
)
as
({\rm I})
and
({\rm II})
.

To apply (
5.22
), we need to pick
\beta^{\prime}
and
m
.
These choices are made in terms of
\xi(\beta)
, as follows.
First, given
0<\beta^{\prime\prime}<\beta^{\prime}<\beta
, we set

m=\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime}),\qquad n=\xi(\beta^{\prime}),\qquad N=\xi(\beta).

(5.23)

Given
\beta^{\prime\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime}
, we use
\mu,\nu\in(0,1]
to denote the ratios

\mu=\frac{m}{n}=\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}{\xi(\beta^{\prime})},\qquad\nu=\frac{n}{N}=\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}{\xi(\beta)}.

(5.24)

We will want to take
\mu,\nu
to be small, but we must have

m=\mu\nu N\geq\xi(0)
in order to have the existence of
\beta^{\prime\prime}

with
m=\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})
.
This can be accomplished as follows.
First, if
\delta\leq\tfrac{1}{4}
and
\beta<\beta(\delta)
, (
4.3
) and (
4.5
) yield

\frac{\xi(\beta)}{\xi(0)}\geq\left(\frac{\chi(\beta)}{\chi(0)}\right)^{\tfrac{1}{2}-\delta}\geq\left(\frac{\chi(\beta)}{\chi(0)}\right)^{1/4}\geq\frac{1}{(1-\beta(1-\delta))^{1/4}},

(5.25)

so,

\frac{\xi(\beta_{0})}{\xi(0)}\geq\frac{1}{(1-(1-\delta)^{2})^{1/4}}\geq\frac{1}{(2\delta)^{1/4}}.

(5.26)

Therefore, given any
\mu,\nu\leq 1
,
we can choose
\delta(\mu,\nu)
to be small enough that

m=\mu\nu\xi(\beta)\geq\mu\nu\xi(\beta_{0})\geq(2\delta)^{1/4}\xi(\beta_{0})\geq\xi(0)
.

For (
5.22
), we also need
|x|>m=\mu\nu\xi(\beta)
.
With later needs in mind, we require that
\nu\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta
.
Then, since we assume that
|x|\geq\eta\xi(\beta)
,
we (more than) ensure that

|x|>m
since
m=\mu\nu N\leq\nu N\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta N
. In the following,
we will first fix
\mu
, and then choose a
\nu
satisfying
\nu\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta

(with further restriction below).
This adds
\eta
-dependence to
\delta
. We also require that
\delta\leq\tfrac{1}{8}(\mu\wedge\nu)^{2}
so that the conclusions of Corollary
4.2
and Proposition
4.3

hold with the pairs
(\beta^{\prime\prime},\beta^{\prime})
and
(\beta^{\prime},\beta)
.

By Proposition
4.5
and (
4.16
), the ratio in the first term on
the right-hand side of (
5.22
) obeys

\frac{\chi_{m}(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}=\frac{\chi_{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}(\beta^{\prime})}{\chi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\leq C_{\textup{stab}}\cdot 2\left(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}\right)^{2}=C_{\textup{stab}}\cdot 2\mu^{2}.

(5.27)

By the assumption that
\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C})
holds,
and by using
\mu n/N=\mu\nu\leq 1
and our assumption

\mathbf{c}\leq 1
in the exponent, we see that

\sup_{y\in\Lambda_{m}(0)}F_{\beta}(x-y)\leq e\cdot\frac{\mathbf{C}}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee(|x|-\mu n)}\right)^{d-2-\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\mathbf{c}\frac{|x|}{\xi(\beta)}\right).

(5.28)

Since
|x|\geq\eta N
, and since
\mu\nu\leq\nu\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta
, we have

|x|-\mu n\geq|x|\left(1-\frac{\mu n}{\eta N}\right)=|x|\left(1-\frac{\mu\nu}{\eta}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}|x|.

(5.29)

Therefore, with the choice

\mu^{2}=\frac{1}{16}\frac{1}{e2^{d-2-\varepsilon}C_{\textup{stab}}2},

(5.30)

the first term of (
5.22
) obeys

({\rm I})\leq\frac{1}{16}\frac{\mathbf{C}}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2-\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\mathbf{c}\frac{|x|}{\xi(\beta)}\right).

(5.31)

To handle the second term on the right-hand side of (
5.22
), we apply
Lemma
5.2
with
f=F_{\beta^{\prime}}
,
g=\mathbb{G}_{Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta},\beta^{\prime}}
,
\xi=\xi(\beta^{\prime})
, and with the
\mu
we chose in (
5.30
).
The hypothesis on
f
is verified by

\mathcal{H}_{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C})
with
c_{1}=\mathbf{C}
,
a=\mathbf{c}
,
\xi=n
, and the hypothesis
on
g
is verified by Theorem
2.10
with

c_{2}=\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}
,
b=\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}}\sqrt{1-Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}}
,
\xi=n
.
It therefore follows from Lemma
5.2
, since
|x|\geq\eta N\geq 2n

(as
\nu\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta
)
and
\|\mathbb{G}_{Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta},\beta^{\prime}}\|_{1}=(1-Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta})^{-1}
, that

\displaystyle({\rm II})\leq\frac{\mathbf{C}}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\left(\frac{2^{d}}{1-Z}e^{-\mathbf{c}|x|/2n}+\mathsf{C}_{\textup{RW}}C_{\mathbf{c},\mu}\left(\frac{n}{|x|}\right)^{\varepsilon}e^{-\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}}\sqrt{1-Z}|x|/2n}\right),

(5.32)

where
Z=Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}
. By (
4.26
) applied to
(\beta^{\prime},\beta)
, we
have

\displaystyle Z\leq 1-\frac{1}{4}\Big(\frac{n}{N}\Big)^{2}.

(5.33)

Since
\mathbf{c}\leq\frac{1}{4}\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}}
, we also find

\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}}\frac{\sqrt{1-Z}}{2n}\geq 4\mathbf{c}\frac{1}{4N}=\frac{\mathbf{c}}{N}.

(5.34)

We use the above, together with
|x|\geq\eta N
, to conclude that
there is a constant
C_{1}=C_{1}(d,\eta,\mathbf{c},\mu)>0
such that

\displaystyle({\rm II})\leq C_{1}\Big(\frac{n}{N}\Big)^{\varepsilon}\frac{\mathbf{C}}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\left(\left(\frac{N}{n}\right)^{2+\varepsilon}e^{-\mathbf{c}|x|/2n}+e^{-\mathbf{c}|x|/N}\right).

(5.35)

Again using
|x|\geq\eta N
, we see that

\frac{|x|}{2n}=\frac{|x|}{N}+\Big(\frac{N}{2n}-1\Big)\frac{|x|}{N}\geq\frac{|x|}{N}+\Big(\frac{N}{2n}-1\Big)\eta,

(5.36)

and hence

\left(\frac{N}{n}\right)^{2+\varepsilon}e^{-\mathbf{c}|x|/2n}\leq e^{-\mathbf{c}|x|/N}e^{\mathbf{c}\eta}\left(\frac{N}{n}\right)^{2+\varepsilon}e^{-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{c}\eta(N/n)}\leq C_{2}(\mathbf{c},\eta,\varepsilon)e^{-\mathbf{c}|x|/N}.

(5.37)

Therefore, by (
5.35
)
and (
5.37
),
there is a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(d,\eta,\mathbf{c},\mu,\varepsilon)>0
such that

({\rm II})\leq C_{3}\nu^{\varepsilon}\frac{\mathbf{C}}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\mathbf{c}\frac{|x|}{N}\right).

(5.38)

The inequality (
5.38
) holds for any
\nu
such that
\nu\leq\frac{1}{2}\eta
.
In terms of our fixed choice of
\mu
in (
5.30
),
we can now choose
\nu
small enough (which entails taking
\delta
small) so that
C_{3}\nu^{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{16}
.

From the bound on
({\rm I})
in (
5.31
), together with the bound on
({\rm II})

in (
5.38
) with our choice of
\nu
, we obtain the desired result
that

F_{\beta}(x)\leq\left(\frac{1}{16}+\frac{1}{16}\right)\frac{\mathbf{C}}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\exp\left(-\mathbf{c}\frac{|x|}{N}\right).

(5.39)

This completes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

The need for
\varepsilon>0
occurs only near the end of
the previous proof, to achieve
C_{3}\nu^{\varepsilon}\leq\frac{1}{16}
.
This is the essential element of the contraction proof for typical scales.
There is no additional need for
\varepsilon
elsewhere in the entire paper.

5.3
Proof of Proposition
3.7
: contraction step for intermediate scales

The proof of Proposition
3.7
uses the following elementary convolution lemma,
whose proof is deferred to Appendix
B
.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
p,a>0
.
For
i=1,2
, suppose that
f_{i}\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{Z}^{d})

satisfy
0\leq f_{i}(x)\leq a(1\vee|x|)^{-p}
for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.
Let
k\geq 1
.
Then, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

(f_{1}*f_{2})(x)\leq\frac{a}{(1\vee|x|)^{p}}\left(\frac{1}{k^{p}}\|f_{1}\|_{1}+2^{p}\sum_{y\in\Lambda_{k|x|}(0)}(f_{1}(y)+f_{2}(y))\right).

(5.40)

Proposition
3.7
establishes an improvement on the bound on
F_{\beta}
for the
intermediate
scales

2\xi(0)\leq|x|\leq\eta\xi(\beta)
.
We do not use
\varepsilon>0
in the proof of Proposition
3.7
—the parameter

\varepsilon
is merely a spectator.

Proof of Proposition
3.7
.

Let
d>2
,
\varepsilon>0
,
\mathbf{C}\geq 16\mathsf{C}
,
\mathbf{c}\leq\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{c}\wedge\tfrac{1}{4}\mathsf{c}_{\textup{RW}}\wedge 1
and let

\delta_{2}=\delta_{2}(\eta,\varepsilon,d)
be given by
Proposition
3.6
.
Our goal is to prove that there exists
\eta\in(0,1)

such that the following is true for every
\beta<\beta(\delta_{2})
: If
\mathcal{H}_{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C})
holds for every
\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta

then, for every
2\xi(0)\leq|x|\leq\eta\xi(\beta)
,

F_{\beta}(x)\leq\frac{1}{6}\frac{\mathbf{C}}{\sigma^{d}}\left(\frac{\sigma}{\sigma\vee|x|}\right)^{d-2-\varepsilon}.

(5.41)

Since
|x|\geq 2\xi(0)=2\sigma
, the above is equivalent to

F_{\beta}(x)\leq\frac{1}{6}\mathbf{C}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}.

(5.42)

Let
\eta\in(0,1)
to be chosen small enough.
Let
\beta<\beta(\delta_{2})
.
Fix
x
with
2\xi(0)\leq|x|\leq\eta\xi(\beta)
.
We may assume that
2\xi(0)\leq\eta\xi(\beta)
, since otherwise there is nothing to prove. Then, since
2/\eta\geq 1
,

\xi(0)\leq\frac{|x|}{\eta}\leq\xi(\beta).

(5.43)

Since
\xi(\beta)
is monotone increasing in

\beta<\beta(\delta_{2})
(by Proposition
4.1
), we can choose

0<\beta^{\prime}\leq\beta
such that

|x|=\eta\xi(\beta^{\prime})
. With this choice, we apply
(

⁢
I
.1

) to obtain

F_{\beta}(x)\leq F_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)+(\beta-\beta^{\prime})(F_{\beta^{\prime}}*F_{\beta})(x).

(5.44)

For the first term on the right-hand side, we
apply Proposition
3.6
at the parameter
\beta^{\prime}
to get

F_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)\leq\frac{1}{8}\mathbf{C}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}.

(5.45)

The second term on the right-hand side of (
5.44
) is

\frac{Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}(F_{\beta^{\prime}}*F_{\beta})(x)\leq\frac{2}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}(F_{\beta^{\prime}}*F_{\beta})(x)

(5.46)

because, by (
4.24
),
Z_{\beta^{\prime},\beta}\leq\tfrac{1}{1-\delta_{2}}\leq 2

since
\delta_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
As a result, to prove (
5.42
) it suffices to show that, if
\eta
is small enough, then

\frac{1}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}(F_{\beta^{\prime}}*F_{\beta})(x)\leq\frac{1}{48}\mathbf{C}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}.

(5.47)

Given that
\mathcal{H}_{\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C})
and
\mathcal{H}_{\beta}(\mathbf{c},\mathbf{C})
hold, the functions

f_{1}=F_{\beta^{\prime}}
and
f_{2}=F_{\beta}
obey the hypothesis of
Lemma
5.4
with
p=d-2-\varepsilon

and
a=\mathbf{C}\sigma^{-2-\varepsilon}
.
Since

f_{1}\leq f_{2}
, this gives, for every
k\geq 1
,

\frac{1}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}(F_{\beta^{\prime}}*F_{\beta})(x)\leq\mathbf{C}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}\left(\frac{1}{k^{d-2-\varepsilon}}+2^{d-2-\varepsilon}\frac{\chi_{k|x|}(\beta)}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\right).

(5.48)

We choose
k=\eta^{-1/2}\geq 1
,
so that
k|x|=k\eta\xi(\beta^{\prime})=\eta^{1/2}\xi(\beta^{\prime})
. Since

\xi(0)\leq\eta^{-1/2}|x|=\eta^{1/2}\xi(\beta^{\prime})\leq\xi(\beta^{\prime}),

(5.49)

there is a

\beta^{\prime\prime}\leq\beta^{\prime}
for which
\eta^{1/2}\xi(\beta^{\prime})=\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})
.
Then, the ratio in the last term on the right-hand side of (
5.48
) satisfies

\frac{\chi_{k|x|}(\beta)}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}=\frac{\chi_{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}(\beta)}{\chi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}\frac{\chi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}{\chi(\beta^{\prime})}\leq C_{\textup{stab}}\left(\frac{\xi(\beta^{\prime\prime})}{\xi(\beta^{\prime})}\right)^{2\frac{1-\delta_{2}}{1+\delta_{2}}}\leq C_{\textup{stab}}\eta^{1/3},

(5.50)

where we successively used Proposition
4.5
,
(
4.3
), and the fact that the exponent in the third member is at least
\frac{2}{3}
because
\delta_{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}
.
We now choose
\eta\in(0,1)
small enough that

2^{d-2-\varepsilon}(\eta^{\frac{1}{2}(d-2-\varepsilon)}+\eta^{1/3})\leq\frac{1}{48}.

(5.51)

With (
5.48
), this completes the proof of (
5.47
), and concludes the proof of the proposition.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

In the proof of Proposition
3.7
, we showed a weak form of pointwise stability: the second term in the right-hand side of (
5.44
) is much smaller than
any
input bound on
F_{\beta}(x)
. More precisely, we proved that, if
\kappa>0
, for

2\xi(0)\leq|x|\leq\eta\xi(\beta^{\prime})
, if
\eta
(and hence
\delta_{2}
) is sufficiently small, we can ensure that

F_{\beta}(x)\leq F_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)+\frac{\kappa}{\sigma^{2}|x|^{d-2}}\left(\frac{|x|}{\sigma}\right)^{\varepsilon}.

(5.52)

This is the essential element in the contraction proof for intermediate scales.

6
Applications

In this section,
we show that our results apply to several statistical mechanical
models above their upper critical dimensions.
The applications are to: self-avoiding walk
for
d>4
, percolation for
d>6
, spin models (Ising, XY,
1
- and
2
-component
|\varphi|^{4}
)
for
d>4
, and lattice trees for
d>8
.
For each of these we:
(1)
define the model,

(2)
verify that its two-point function satisfies Definition
1.3
,

(3)
verify Assumption
I
, and
(4)
verify Assumption
II
.
Throughout Section
6
, the kernel
J
is any admissible kernel as in Definition
1.1
(i)
–
(ii)
.
Definition
1.1
(iii)
does not play a role in Section
6
, except for lattice trees in Section
6.7
(it is also used indirectly in Section
6.8

via application of Theorem
1.5
).

Most of the analysis in Section
6
is well-known from literature going back to the
1980s. We include it for clarity and completeness, and to illustrate
the parallels between the various models.
Although we work on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
because that is the setting of our
results, the verification of the bounds of Assumptions
I
and
II
presented here apply to general transitive graphs. If the model has a small parameter as part of its definition, then
the nearest-neighbour
J
of (
1.11
) can be used.
If there is no small parameter in the definition of the model, then the verification
of Assumption
II
requires us to use spread-out models with sufficiently large
\sigma_{J}
.

The verification of Assumption
II
is deferred to Section
6.8

for the sake of efficiency, since it is similar for all models under
consideration.

6.1
Self-avoiding walk

6.1.1
The model

Let
d\geq 1
.
Detailed introductions to the self-avoiding walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
can be found in

[
68
,
12
]
.
To define the model, we introduce the
repulsion parameter

\lambda\in[0,1]
. Since
\lambda
is fixed, we omit it from the notation.
For
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we write
x\sim y
if
J_{y-x}>0
. An
n
-
step

walk
from
x
to
y
is a sequence
(\gamma(i))_{0\leq i\leq n}
with
\gamma(0)=x
,
\gamma(n)=y
, and
\gamma(i)\sim\gamma(i+1)
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{0}
be the set of all walks in
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
with
x_{0}=0
. We denote the length of an
n
-step walk
\gamma
by
|\gamma|=n
.

For
\gamma\in\mathcal{W}_{0}
,
0\leq s<t\leq|\gamma|
, and
\beta\geq 0
, we define

\displaystyle U_{s,t}(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=-\lambda\mathds{1}_{\gamma(s)=\gamma(t)},

(6.1)

\displaystyle\rho(\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{0\leq s<t\leq|\gamma|}\big(1+U_{s,t}(\gamma)\big),

(6.2)

\displaystyle(\beta J)^{\gamma}

\displaystyle:=\prod_{0\leq i\leq|\gamma|-1}\beta J_{\gamma(i+1)-\gamma(i)}.

(6.3)

The
two-point function
is defined, for every
\beta\geq 0
and every

x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
by

G_{\beta}(x):=\sum_{\gamma:0\rightarrow x}(\beta J)^{\gamma}\rho(\gamma).

(6.4)

When
\lambda=0
, we recover the Green function of the random walk with step distribution
J
.
The case
\lambda=1
is the
strictly
self-avoiding walk model.
When
\lambda\in(0,1)
, we have the
weakly
self-avoiding walk
(also known as the
Domb–Joyce model
).

We define

c_{n}:=\sum_{\gamma\in\mathcal{W}_{0}:|\gamma|=n}J^{\gamma}\rho(\gamma)
.
This sequence satisfies the inequality
c_{m+n}\leq c_{m}c_{n}
, from which we conclude
by Fekete’s Lemma (see
[
68
, Lemma 1.2.2]
) that
c_{n}^{1/n}
approaches a
limit

\lim_{n\to\infty}c_{n}^{1/n}=\inf_{n\geq 1}c_{n}^{1/n}.

(6.5)

This limit can be shown to lie in
(0,\infty)
.
Its reciprocal is defined to
be the
critical point

\beta_{c}
.
The
susceptibility
is defined by

\chi(\beta)=\sum_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}G_{\beta}(x)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\beta^{n}.

(6.6)

By (
6.5
),
\beta_{c}
is the radius of convergence of
\chi
. In particular,
G_{\beta}(x)
is finite for
\beta\in[0,\beta_{c})
.
Also, since
c_{n}\geq\beta_{c}^{-n}
, we have
\chi(\beta)\geq(1-\beta/\beta_{c})^{-1}
for
\beta\leq\beta_{c}
.

6.1.2
Verification of Definition
1.3

We verify the conditions imposed by Definition
1.3
:

(i)

(Initial condition.) It is immediate from the definition that
G_{0}=\delta_{0}
.

(ii)

(Regularity.) For every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, the function

\beta\in[0,\beta_{c})\mapsto G_{\beta}(x)
is a power series in
\beta
with positive
coefficients, so it is monotone and differentiable.

(iii)

(Symmetry.) Since
J
is
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-symmetric, so is
G_{\beta}
.

(iv)

(Exponential decay.)
Fix
\beta\in[0,\beta_{c})
and let
\beta_{1}=\frac{1}{2}(\beta+\beta_{c})
.
A walk from
0
to
x
must take at least
|x|/R_{j}
steps, so

G_{\beta}(x)\leq\sum_{n=|x|/R_{j}}c_{n}\beta^{n}\leq\Big(\frac{\beta}{\beta_{1}}\Big)^{|x|/R_{J}}\chi(\beta_{1}).

(6.7)

Since
\beta<\beta_{1}
, the right-hand side decays exponentially in
|x|
.

(v)

(Limit as
\beta\nearrow\beta_{c}
when
\beta_{c}<\infty
.)
By the Monotone Convergence Theorem,

\lim_{\beta\uparrow\beta_{c}}G_{\beta}(x)=G_{\beta_{c}}(x)
.

6.1.3
Verification of Assumption
I

Proof of (

⁢
I
.1

).

For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
b^{n}-a^{n}=(b-a)\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}b^{k}a^{n-1-k}
.
As a result,

\displaystyle G_{\beta}(x)-G_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma:0\rightarrow x}\big((\beta J)^{\gamma}-(\beta^{\prime}J)^{\gamma}\big)\rho(\gamma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\gamma:0\rightarrow x}\left((\beta-\beta^{\prime})\sum_{k=0}^{|\gamma|-1}(\beta^{\prime}J)^{\gamma[0:k]}J_{\gamma(k+1)-\gamma(k)}(\beta J)^{\gamma[k+1:|\gamma|]}\right)\rho(\gamma)

\displaystyle=(\beta-\beta^{\prime})\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1}:0\rightarrow u\\
\gamma_{2}:v\rightarrow x\end{subarray}}(\beta^{\prime}J)^{\gamma_{1}}J_{v-u}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{2}}\rho(\gamma_{1}\circ(uv)\circ\gamma_{2}).

(6.8)

By definition,
\rho(\gamma_{1}\circ(uv)\circ\gamma_{2})\leq\rho(\gamma_{1})\rho(\gamma_{2})
, so

\sum_{\gamma_{2}:v\rightarrow x}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{2}}\rho(\gamma_{1}\circ(uv)\circ\gamma_{2})\leq\rho(\gamma_{1})G_{\beta}(x-v),

(6.9)

and therefore

\displaystyle G_{\beta}(x)-G_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle\leq(\beta-\beta^{\prime})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\\
\gamma:0\rightarrow u\end{subarray}}(\beta^{\prime}J)^{\gamma}J_{v-u}\rho(\gamma)G_{\beta}(x-v)

\displaystyle=(\beta-\beta^{\prime})(G_{\beta^{\prime}}*J*G_{\beta})(x).

(6.10)

This proves (

⁢
I
.1

).
Figure
2
illustrates the upper bound in (
6.10
).
∎

Figure 2:
Diagrammatic representations for (
6.10
) (left)
and (
6.17
) (right). On the left,
the grey path has parameter
\beta^{\prime}
and the black has
\beta
. The dotted lines represent a single-step walk.

We define the
open bubble diagram

B^{\rm o}(\beta):=(G_{\beta}*J*G_{\beta})(0).

(6.11)

and verify the lower differential inequality (

⁢
I
.2

) with

H_{\beta}(x)=\lambda B^{\rm o}(\beta)\delta_{0}(x).

(6.12)

By definition,
H_{0}=0
,
H_{\beta}(x)=H_{\beta}(-x)
, and
H_{\beta}
is increasing
and continuous on
[0,\beta_{c})
, as required by Assumption
I
.
Also, since
H_{\beta}
is supported on
\{0\}
, it certainly decays exponentially.

Since we have verified (

⁢
I
.1

),
it follows from (
1.13
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}G_{\beta}

\displaystyle\leq G_{\beta}*J*G_{\beta}.

(6.13)

We will use this in the proof of the complementary lower bound

\partial_{\beta}G_{\beta}\geq G_{\beta}*J*G_{\beta}-G_{\beta}*H_{\beta}*G_{\beta},

(6.14)

which is (

⁢
I
.2

).

Proof of (

⁢
I
.2

).

By writing
|\gamma|=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{i=1}^{|\gamma|}\mathds{1}_{\gamma(i)=z}
,
we see that

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}G_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma:0\to x\\
|\gamma|\geq 1\end{subarray}}\beta^{|\gamma|-1}J^{\gamma}\rho(\gamma)\sum_{i=1}^{|\gamma|}\mathds{1}_{\gamma(i)=v}

\displaystyle=\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1}:0\rightarrow u\\
\gamma_{2}:v\rightarrow x\end{subarray}}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{1}}J_{v-u}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{2}}\rho(\gamma_{1}\circ(uv)\circ\gamma_{2}).

(6.15)

We apply the inequality

\rho(\gamma_{1}\circ(uv)\circ\gamma_{2})\geq\rho(\gamma_{1})\rho(\gamma_{2})-\lambda\rho(\gamma_{1})\rho(\gamma_{2})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i\leq|\gamma_{1}|\\
0\leq j\leq|\gamma_{2}|\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}_{\gamma_{1}(i)=\gamma_{2}(j)}.

(6.16)

Insertion of the first term on the right-hand side
into (
6.1.3
) gives the first term

(G_{\beta}*J*G_{\beta})(x)
in our desired bound
(
6.14
).

To arrive at the remaining term in (
6.14
),
we decompose according to the common value
z
of
\gamma_{1}(i)
and
\gamma_{2}(j)
. The
subtracted term in (
6.16
) becomes

\displaystyle\lambda\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1}:0\rightarrow u\\
\gamma_{2}:v\rightarrow x\end{subarray}}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{1}}J_{v-u}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{2}}\rho(\gamma_{1})\rho(\gamma_{2})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq i\leq|\gamma_{1}|\\
0\leq j\leq|\gamma_{2}|\end{subarray}}\mathds{1}_{\gamma_{1}(i)=\gamma_{2}(j)}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\sum_{u,v,z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\gamma_{1}:0\rightarrow z\\
\gamma_{2}:z\rightarrow u\\
\gamma_{3}:v\rightarrow z\\
\gamma_{4}:z\rightarrow x\end{subarray}}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2}}J_{v-u}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{3}\circ\gamma_{4}}\rho(\gamma_{1}\circ\gamma_{2})\rho(\gamma_{3}\circ\gamma_{4})

\displaystyle\leq\lambda\sum_{u,v,z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}G_{\beta}(z)G_{\beta}(u-z)J_{v-u}G_{\beta}(z-v)G_{\beta}(x-z)

\displaystyle=\lambda B^{\rm o}(\beta)\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}G_{\beta}(z)G_{\beta}(x-z)=\lambda B^{\rm o}(\beta)(G_{\beta}*G_{\beta})(x).

(6.17)

The right-hand side is exactly
(G_{\beta}*H_{\beta}*G_{\beta})(x)
with
H_{\beta}

given by (
6.12
).
∎

6.2
Continuous-time weakly self-avoiding walk

We now verify Assumption
I
for the continuous-time weakly
self-avoiding walk. Since the argument closely follows that of the
(discrete-time) weakly self-avoiding walk, we only sketch the details.

Let
d\geq 1
.
The study of the continuous-time weakly self-avoiding walk on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}

(also known as the
discrete Edwards model
)
goes back to
[
14
]
. The model is interesting even for
d=1
, e.g.,
[
61
]
.

To define the model, we first consider the continuous-time random walk
X
on
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
which takes steps
according to an admissible kernel
J
, with
{\rm Exp}(1)
holding times.
In other words, the random time spent at a vertex before making a next step
has density
e^{-t}\mathrm{d}t
, and the holding times at each step are independent
of each other and of the choice of next vertex.
For a walk with trace
\gamma
taking
|\gamma|

steps, let
T_{0},\ldots,T_{|\gamma|}
denote the independent
{\rm Exp}(1)
holding
times, and let
T=\sum_{i=0}^{|\gamma|}T_{i}
denote the total time of
\gamma
.

The
local time
at a vertex
y
visited by
\gamma
is the random
variable

L_{y,\gamma}=\sum_{i=0}^{|\gamma|}T_{i}\mathds{1}_{\gamma(i)=y}=\int_{0}^{T}\mathds{1}_{X(t)=y}\mathrm{d}t.

(6.18)

The
intersection local time

I(\gamma):=\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}L_{y,\gamma}^{2}=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{T}\mathds{1}_{X(s)=X(t)}\mathrm{d}s\mathrm{d}t

(6.19)

gives an indication of the total time that
the walk
\gamma
spends intersecting itself.
Given
\lambda>0
, we define a weight
\rho(\gamma)
by

\rho(\gamma):=\mathbb{E}\,(e^{-\lambda I(\gamma)}),

(6.20)

where
\mathbb{E}
denotes the expectation over the holding times.

When
y
is visited
n_{y}
times by the walk
\gamma
, the local time
L_{y,\gamma}
has a
{\rm Gamma}(n_{y},1)
distribution.
Therefore, in terms of the
measure on
(\mathbb{R}^{+})^{\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\nu_{\gamma}(\mathbf{t})

\displaystyle=\prod_{v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\left(\mathrm{1}_{n_{v}(\gamma)=0}\delta_{0}(t_{v})\mathrm{d}t_{v}+\mathrm{1}_{n_{v}(\gamma)\geq 1}\frac{t_{v}^{n_{v}(\gamma)-1}}{(n_{v}(\gamma)-1)!}\mathrm{d}t_{v}\right),

(6.21)

the weight of
\gamma
is equal to

\rho(\gamma)=\mathbb{E}\,(e^{-\lambda I(\gamma)})=\int e^{-\sum_{y}t_{y}}e^{-\lambda\sum_{y}t_{y}^{2}}\mathrm{d}\nu_{\gamma}(\mathbf{t}).

(6.22)

The
two-point function
is defined by

G_{\beta}(x):=\sum_{\gamma:0\to x}(\beta J)^{\gamma}\rho(\gamma).

(6.23)

Verification of Definition
1.3
.

This is identical to the verification for the self-avoiding walk model in Section
6.1.2
. Indeed, in Section
6.1.2
, we did not use the precise form of
\rho(\gamma)
, but only the fact that it does not depend on
\beta
. This remains true in
(
6.23
).

Proof of (

⁢
I
.1

).

Suppose that
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\circ(uv)\circ\gamma_{2}
.
The intersection local time obeys the inequality

I(\gamma)\geq I(\gamma_{1})+I(\gamma_{2}).

(6.24)

Since the holding times of
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
are independent, so are the random variables
I(\gamma_{1})
and
I(\gamma_{2})
.
This leads to the inequality
\rho(\gamma_{1}\circ(uv)\circ\gamma_{2})\leq\rho(\gamma_{1})\rho(\gamma_{2})
that was used to verify Assumption
I
in
Section
6.1.3
.
Then (

⁢
I
.1

) follows exactly as in Section
6.1.3
.
∎

Proof of (

⁢
I
.2

).

We start from (
6.1.3
), with the new interpretation of the weight
\rho
. Let
\gamma=\gamma_{1}\circ(uv)\circ\gamma_{2}
.
The error in the upper bound
\rho(\gamma)\leq\rho(\gamma_{1})\rho(\gamma_{2})

used to prove (

⁢
I
.1

) arises from

\displaystyle e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{1})}e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{2})}-e^{-\lambda I(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{1})}e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{2})}\Big[1-e^{-\lambda[I(\gamma)-(I(\gamma_{1})+I(\gamma_{2}))]}\Big]

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{1})}e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{2})}\lambda[I(\gamma)-(I(\gamma_{1})+I(\gamma_{2}))].

(6.25)

By definition,
L_{z,\gamma}=L_{z,\gamma_{1}}+L_{z,\gamma_{2}}
. Therefore,

I(\gamma)-(I(\gamma_{1})+I(\gamma_{2}))=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(L_{z,\gamma_{1}}+L_{z,\gamma_{2}})^{2}-\left(L_{z,\gamma_{1}}^{2}+L_{z,\gamma_{2}}^{2}\right)=2\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}L_{z,\gamma_{1}}L_{z,\gamma_{2}}.

(6.26)

As a consequence,

\rho(\gamma_{1})\rho(\gamma_{2})-\rho(\gamma)\leq 2\lambda\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big(e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{1})}L_{z,\gamma_{1}}\big)\Big)\Big(\mathbb{E}\big(e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{2})}L_{z,\gamma_{2}}\big)\Big).

(6.27)

Now, we sum over
\gamma
as in (
6.1.3
). The result is

\displaystyle(G_{\beta}*J*G_{\beta})(x)-\partial_{\beta}G_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\lambda\sum_{u,v,z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\sum_{\gamma_{1}:0\rightarrow u}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{1}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big(e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{1})}L_{z,\gamma_{1}}\big)\Big)J_{v-u}\sum_{\gamma_{2}:v\rightarrow x}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{2}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big(e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{2})}L_{z,\gamma_{2}}\big)\Big).

(6.28)

We appeal to
[
17
, Lemma 2.1]
to see that

\sum_{\gamma_{1}:0\rightarrow u}(\beta J)^{\gamma_{1}}\Big(\mathbb{E}\big(e^{-\lambda I(\gamma_{1})}L_{z,\gamma_{1}}\big)\Big)\leq G_{\beta}(z)G_{\beta}(u-z),

(6.29)

and similarly for the sum over
\gamma_{2}
. We therefore obtain

\displaystyle(G_{\beta}*J*G_{\beta})(x)-\partial_{\beta}G_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle\quad\leq 2\lambda\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}G_{\beta}(z)G_{\beta}(x-z)\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}G_{\beta}(u-z)J_{v-u}G_{\beta}(z-v).

(6.30)

By replacing
u,v
by
u-x,v-z
, this leads to

\displaystyle(G_{\beta}*J*G_{\beta})(x)-\partial_{\beta}G_{\beta}(x)\leq 2\lambda(G_{\beta}*H_{\beta}*G_{\beta})(x)

(6.31)

with

H_{\beta}(x)=2\lambda\delta_{0}(x)(G_{\beta}*J*G_{\beta})(0).

(6.32)

This completes the proof.
∎

6.3
Bernoulli percolation

6.3.1
The model

For an introduction to percolation theory, see
[
40
]
.
Let
d\geq 2
.
We consider Bernoulli bond percolation on the infinite graph whose vertex
set is
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and whose edge set
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{J}
consists of pairs
\{x,y\}
with

J_{y-x}>0
. Let
\beta\in[0,(\max_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}J_{x})^{-1}]
.
Edges are independently open with probability
\beta J_{y-x}

and otherwise are closed. We write
\{x\xleftrightarrow{}y\}

for the event that
x
and
y
are connected by a path consisting of open bonds,
and define the
two-point function

G_{\beta}(x,y)=G_{\beta}(y-x):=\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[x\xleftrightarrow{}y].

(6.33)

6.3.2
Verification of Definition
1.3

For
d\geq 2
,
the properties listed in Definition
1.3
are standard facts about
percolation
[
40
]
. The critical value
\beta_{c}
separates the subcritical regime,
where
G_{\beta}
decays exponentially, from the supercritical regime, where
there is a positive probability for the existence of an infinite connected
cluster
[
70
,
2
]
. We assume familiarity with two basic techniques: the BK inequality (van den Berg–Kesten) and Russo’s formula
[
40
, Chapter 2]
.

6.3.3
Verification of Assumption
I

Proof of (

⁢
I
.1

).

A version of (

⁢
I
.1

) appeared in
[
49
, Lemma 2.4]
.
We use the standard increasing coupling
\mathbb{P}
, as follows.
First, we assign independent uniform random variables
\eta_{u,v}
in
[0,1]
to each edge
\{u,v\}
.
Given a realisation of these random variables, and given
\beta\in[0,(\max_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}J_{x})^{-1}]
, we define a percolation configuration
\omega_{\beta}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathcal{E}}
as follows:
\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=1
if

\eta_{u,v}<\beta J_{v-u}
(the bond
\{u,v\}
is
open
), and otherwise
\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=0
. Below, we also view
\omega_{\beta}
as a subgraph of
(\mathbb{Z}^{d},\mathcal{E})
of vertex set
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
and edge set
\{\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{E}:\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=1\}
. We also define
\{x\xleftrightarrow{A\>}y\}

to be the event that
x
is connected to
y

by a path which does not pass through any vertex in
A^{c}
.
By definition of the coupling, if
\beta<\beta^{\prime}
then
\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}
is a subgraph of
\omega_{\beta}
, and, for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
,

G_{\beta}(x)-G_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)=\mathbb{P}[\{0\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta}\>}x\}\setminus\{0\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}\>}x\}].

(6.34)

For
\beta\in[0,(\max_{x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}J_{x})^{-1}]
and
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we define
\mathcal{C}_{\beta}(z)
to be the cluster of
z
in
\omega_{\beta}
. For
\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{E}
, we define
\omega^{\{u,v\}}_{\beta}
to be the percolation configuration obtained from
\omega_{\beta}
by setting
\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=0
,
and we define
\mathcal{C}_{\beta}^{\{u,v\}}(z)
to be the cluster of
z
in
\omega_{\beta}^{\{u,v\}}
.
For fixed
\beta^{\prime}<\beta
and
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we claim that
the event on the right-hand side of (
6.34
) satisfies

\{0\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta}\>}x\}\setminus\{0\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}\>}x\}\\
\subset\bigcup_{\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{E}}\Big\{u\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)\Big\}\cap\{\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}(\{u,v\})=0,\>\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=1\}\cap\Big\{v\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)\>}x\Big\}.

(6.35)

The claim is justified as follows.
For every configuration in
\{0\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta}\>}x\}\setminus\{0\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}\>}x\}
, there must exist
u\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)
and
v\notin\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)
such that
\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=1
and
v
is connected to
x
in
\omega_{\beta}
without using the vertices in
\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)
. This can be seen by exploring an open self-avoiding path from
0
to
x
in
\omega_{\beta}
and marking its edge
\{u,v\}
for which
u
is the last vertex of
\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)
visited by this path.

Since
\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}(\{u,v\})=0
, we can replace
\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)
by
\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\{u,v\}}(0)
in (
6.35
). A union bound then gives

G_{\beta}(x)-G_{\beta^{\prime}}(x)\\
\leq\sum_{\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{E}}\mathbb{P}\Big[\Big\{u\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\{u,v\}}(0)\Big\}\cap\{\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}(\{u,v\})=0,\>\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=1\}\cap\Big\{v\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\{u,v\}}(0)\>}x\Big\}\Big].

(6.36)

Given
\{u,v\}\in\mathcal{E}
, we condition on the cluster

\mathcal{C}^{{}^{\{u,v\}}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)
. This gives

\mathbb{P}\Big[\Big\{u\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\{u,v\}}(0)\Big\}\cap\{\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}(\{u,v\})=0,\>\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=1\}\cap\Big\{v\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\{u,v\}}(0)\>}x\Big\}\Big]\\
=\sum_{C\ni 0,u}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{C}^{{}^{\{u,v\}}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)=C]\mathbb{P}[\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}(\{u,v\})=0,\>\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=1]\mathbb{P}[v\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta}\setminus C\>}x],

(6.37)

because the three events on the right hand side of (
6.37
) depend on disjoint sets of edges and are therefore independent.
By definition of the coupling,
\mathbb{P}[\omega_{\beta^{\prime}}(\{u,v\})=0,\>\omega_{\beta}(\{u,v\})=1]=\mathbb{P}[\eta_{u,v}\in[\beta^{\prime}J_{v-u},\beta J_{v-u})]=\beta-\beta^{\prime}
,
and by inclusion of events,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[v\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta}\setminus C\>}x]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}[v\xleftrightarrow{\omega_{\beta}\>}x]=G_{\beta}(x-v),

(6.38)

\displaystyle\sum_{C\ni 0,u}\mathbb{P}[\mathcal{C}^{\{u,v\}}_{\beta^{\prime}}(0)

\displaystyle=C]=\mathbb{P}[u\in\mathcal{C}_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\{u,v\}}(0)]\leq G_{\beta^{\prime}}(u).

(6.39)

The last three observations, combined with (
6.36
)–(
6.37
),
complete the proof.
∎

Figure 3:
Depiction of
(G_{\beta}*H_{\beta}*G_{\beta})(x)
for percolation.
Lines represent two-point functions and the gap between
u,v
represents
a factor
J_{v-u}
.
The vertices
u,v,y,z
are summed over
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
.

We will prove the lower differential inequality (

⁢
I
.2

) with

H_{\beta}:=(G_{\beta}*J*G_{\beta})\cdot G_{\beta}.

(6.40)

By definition,
H_{0}(x)=J_{x}\delta_{0}(x)=0
, and
H_{\beta}(-x)=H_{\beta}(x)
for every
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}

and all
\beta\in[0,\beta_{c})
.
Since
G_{\beta}
decays exponentially for
\beta\in(0,\beta_{c})
,
the same is true for
H_{\beta}
.

Proof of (

⁢
I
.2

).

The lower differential inequality (

⁢
I
.2

), with

H_{\beta}
given by (
6.40
),
is a minor modification of a differential inequality for the susceptibility
that was first proved in
[
6
]
before the advent of the BK inequality.
For a more modern proof, see
[
82
, Proposition 9.11]
.
Although we have no new insights for the proof, we provide the argument for the sake of completeness. Let
0\leq\beta<\beta_{c}
.
By Russo’s formula,

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta}G_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}J_{v-u}\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\{u,v\}\text{ closed and pivotal for }0\xleftrightarrow{}x]

\displaystyle=\sum_{u,v\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}J_{v-u}\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\{0\xleftrightarrow{}u\}\cap\{v\xleftrightarrow{}x\}\cap\{u\xleftrightarrow{}v\}^{c}]

(6.41)

(to focus on the key ideas, we do not address the subtlety that Russo’s formula initially applies only to events depending on
finitely many bonds).
It suffices to prove that

\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\{0\xleftrightarrow{}u\}\cap\{v\xleftrightarrow{}x\}\cap\{u\xleftrightarrow{}v\}^{c}]\geq G_{\beta}(u)G_{\beta}(x-v)\\
-\sum_{y,z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}G_{\beta}(z)G_{\beta}(u-z)G_{\beta}(y-z)G_{\beta}(y-v)G_{\beta}(x-y),

(6.42)

since this yields

\partial_{\beta}G_{\beta}(x)\geq(G_{\beta}*(J-H_{\beta})*G_{\beta})(x).

(6.43)

Connections arising in the proof of (
6.42
)
are illustrated in Figure
3
.

Given a set
A
of vertices, we define
\{x\xleftrightarrow{}y\textup{ using }A\}
to be the event that
x
is
connected to
y
and that every path that realises the connection must contain a vertex
in
A
.

To prove (
6.42
),
we condition on the cluster
\mathcal{C}(u)
of
u
.
This gives,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\{0\xleftrightarrow{}u\}\cap\{v\xleftrightarrow{}x\}\cap\{u\xleftrightarrow{}v\}^{c}]=\sum_{C\ni 0,u}\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\mathcal{C}(u)=C]\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[v\xleftrightarrow{C^{c}\>}x],

(6.44)

where we used the facts that
\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[v\xleftrightarrow{C^{c}\>}x]=0
if
v\in C
, and that the events
\{\mathcal{C}(u)=C\}
and
\{v\xleftrightarrow{C^{c}\>}x\}
are independent.
It follows by definition of the events that

\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[v\xleftrightarrow{C^{c}\>}x]=G_{\beta}(x-v)-\mathbb{P}[v\xleftrightarrow{}x\textup{ using }C].

(6.45)

If
\{v\xleftrightarrow{}x\textup{ using }C\}
occurs, there must exist
y\in C
such that
\{v\xleftrightarrow{}y\}\circ\{y\xleftrightarrow{}x\}
occurs.
Therefore, by the BK inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[v\xleftrightarrow{C^{c}\>}x]\geq G_{\beta}(x-v)-\sum_{y\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\mathds{1}_{y\in C}G_{\beta}(y-v)G_{\beta}(x-y).

(6.46)

We insert (
6.46
) into (
6.44
) and obtain

\mathbb{P}_{\beta}[\{0\xleftrightarrow{}u\}\cap\{v\xleftrightarrow{}x\}\cap\{u\xleftrightarrow{}v\}^{c}]\geq G_{\beta}(u)G_{\beta}(x-v)-\\
